Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t really feel bad about it though—I mean, I feel lighter on my feet when I play basketball, and at the garage I’m not feeling as weighed down moving around cars and equipment and all that. So… I guess they were concerned, but I’m not sure it’s as big a deal as they made it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, but yeah, I don’t like it. If I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Like I’m gonna be slower getting around at work, crawling around cars, lifting stuff… and on the basketball court too. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because of your concerns about weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I just keep it simple, like coffee in the morning, and then maybe later I’ll have a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. At the shop we run late a lot anyway—someone always drops off a car right before closing, you know how that goes—and then at home it’s busy too, so I just… I feel better sticking to my own rules with food. It feels more controlled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a normal or large amount of food would make you feel out of control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Like if I sit down and eat a full plate, especially a big meal, I start feeling like I’ve let things get away from me. So most days I keep it pretty basic—coffee in the morning, then maybe later a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around.\n\nThe shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, so it’s easier to just stick to my own simple rules. I know where I’m at that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit what you eat more about feeling in control or about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably both, but if I’m being honest, the weight thing is what kicks it off. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, sometimes I can see why somebody might say I’m lean, but “too thin” sounds like… I don’t know, like I’d be falling apart or something.\n\nPeople are worried, I get that. But I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff there, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like last weekend we had a pickup game and I was moving fine, so in my head it’s hard not to think people are making a bigger deal out of it than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I mean… healthy, I guess I’d say yes, or at least not as unhealthy as everyone keeps acting. I know people are worried, and I’m not trying to be a jerk about it, but I still get up, go to the shop, do my hours, come home and handle whatever needs doing around the house.\n\nAnd I’m still playing ball on weekends, too. Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that, you know? So to me it seems like people are maybe making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess what I see in the mirror feels pretty much like me. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, sometimes I can see why somebody might say I’m lean, but “too thin” sounds like… I don’t know, like I’d be falling apart or something.\n\nPeople are worried, I get that. But I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff there, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like last weekend we had a pickup game and I was moving fine, so in my head it’s hard not to think people are making a bigger deal out of it than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I mean… healthy, I guess I’d say yes, or at least not as unhealthy as everyone keeps acting. I know people are worried, and I’m not trying to be a jerk about it, but I still get up, go to the shop, do my hours, come home and handle whatever needs doing around the house.\n\nAnd I’m still playing ball on weekends, too. Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that, you know? So to me it seems like people are maybe making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess what I see in the mirror feels pretty much like me. I don’t look at it and think, “Oh, that’s a problem,” you know?\n\nI know people are worried, and I’m not saying they’re making it up, but it feels like they’re making more of it than it is. I still get myself to the garage, do my work, come home and take care of whatever needs doing, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like, if I can still do all that, it’s hard for me to see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight affects how you see your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t really think about it like that. When I look at myself, I just see me, you know? I know everyone’s worried and saying stuff, but I keep thinking if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to keep doing my normal things.\n\nLike, I still show up at the shop, get through the day, come home and deal with whatever needs doing around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. Actually, last weekend the guys were joking about how I still run around like I’m younger than I am, so… I don’t know. Like if I sit down and eat a full plate, especially a big meal, I start feeling like I’ve let things get away from me. I know where I’m at that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit what you eat more about feeling in control or about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably both, but if I’m being honest, the weight thing is what kicks it off. It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. At the shop we run late a lot anyway—someone always drops off a car right before closing, you know how that goes—and then at home it’s busy too, so I just… I feel better sticking to my own rules with food. It feels more controlled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a normal or large amount of food would make you feel out of control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Like if I sit down and eat a full plate, especially a big meal, I start feeling like I’ve let things get away from me. So most days I keep it pretty basic—coffee in the morning, then maybe later a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around.\n\nThe shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, so it’s easier to just stick to my own simple rules. I know where I’m at that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit what you eat more about feeling in control or about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably both, but if I’m being honest, the weight thing is what kicks it off. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, sometimes I can see why somebody might say I’m lean, but “too thin” sounds like… I don’t know, like I’d be falling apart or something.\n\nPeople are worried, I get that. But I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff there, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like last weekend we had a pickup game and I was moving fine, so in my head it’s hard not to think people are making a bigger deal out of it than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I mean… healthy, I guess I’d say yes, or at least not as unhealthy as everyone keeps acting. I know people are worried, and I’m not trying to be a jerk about it, but I still get up, go to the shop, do my hours, come home and handle whatever needs doing around the house.\n\nAnd I’m still playing ball on weekends, too. Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that, you know? So to me it seems like people are maybe making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess what I see in the mirror feels pretty much like me. I don’t look at it and think, “Oh, that’s a problem,” you know?\n\nI know people are worried, and I’m not saying they’re making it up, but it feels like they’re making more of it than it is. I still get myself to the garage, do my work, come home and take care of whatever needs doing, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like, if I can still do all that, it’s hard for me to see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight affects how you see your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t really think about it like that. When I look at myself, I just see me, you know? I know everyone’s worried and saying stuff, but I keep thinking if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to keep doing my normal things.\n\nLike, I still show up at the shop, get through the day, come home and deal with whatever needs doing around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. Actually, last weekend the guys were joking about how I still run around like I’m younger than I am, so… I don’t know. It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. People keep saying I’m too low or that they’re worried, and I hear them, I do.\n\nBut then I’m like, I still get up and go to work at the shop, I still do what needs doing around the house. I was fixing this one guy’s truck the other day—thing was a mess, honestly—and I got through that fine. And I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to believe it’s as bad as people are making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you appear thinner to yourself than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t really think I look thinner than I am. I mean, people keep saying they’re worried, and I hear it, but I also feel like they might be making too much out of it.\n\nLike, I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. If I was really that bad off, I feel like I wouldn’t be doing all that, you know?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, sometimes I can see why somebody might say I’m lean, but “too thin” sounds like… I don’t know, like I’d be falling apart or something.\n\nPeople are worried, I get that. But I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff there, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like last weekend we had a pickup game and I was moving fine, so in my head it’s hard not to think people are making a bigger deal out of it than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I mean… healthy, I guess I’d say yes, or at least not as unhealthy as everyone keeps acting. I know people are worried, and I’m not trying to be a jerk about it, but I still get up, go to the shop, do my hours, come home and handle whatever needs doing around the house.\n\nAnd I’m still playing ball on weekends, too. Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that, you know? So to me it seems like people are maybe making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess what I see in the mirror feels pretty much like me. I don’t look at it and think, “Oh, that’s a problem,” you know?\n\nI know people are worried, and I’m not saying they’re making it up, but it feels like they’re making more of it than it is. I still get myself to the garage, do my work, come home and take care of whatever needs doing, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like, if I can still do all that, it’s hard for me to see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight affects how you see your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t really think about it like that. When I look at myself, I just see me, you know? I know everyone’s worried and saying stuff, but I keep thinking if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to keep doing my normal things.\n\nLike, I still show up at the shop, get through the day, come home and deal with whatever needs doing around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. Actually, last weekend the guys were joking about how I still run around like I’m younger than I am, so… I don’t know. It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. People keep saying I’m too low or that they’re worried, and I hear them, I do.\n\nBut then I’m like, I still get up and go to work at the shop, I still do what needs doing around the house. I was fixing this one guy’s truck the other day—thing was a mess, honestly—and I got through that fine. And I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to believe it’s as bad as people are making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you appear thinner to yourself than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t really think I look thinner than I am. I mean, people keep saying they’re worried, and I hear it, but I also feel like they might be making too much out of it.\n\nLike, I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. If I was really that bad off, I feel like I wouldn’t be doing all that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the reason people are worried about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, probably that’s part of it. They see the weight or how I look and they get nervous, I guess. But I still feel like they’re making it bigger than it is.\n\nI’m still at the shop every day, you know, getting under cars, dealing with customers, all that. And at home there’s always something that needs fixing or doing, so it’s not like I’m just sitting around unable to function. Plus I still play ball on weekends—last time we were out there one of the guys was complaining more than I was, and he’s younger than me.\n\nSo… yeah, I know they’re worried, but I guess I don’t really see it as being that serious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m not really sure how to answer that. Like, I look in the mirror and I just see… me, I guess. I don’t know if that matches some picture in my head or not.

Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, sometimes I can see why somebody might say I’m lean, but “too thin” sounds like… I don’t know, like I’d be falling apart or something.\n\nPeople are worried, I get that. But I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff there, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like last weekend we had a pickup game and I was moving fine, so in my head it’s hard not to think people are making a bigger deal out of it than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I mean… healthy, I guess I’d say yes, or at least not as unhealthy as everyone keeps acting. I know people are worried, and I’m not trying to be a jerk about it, but I still get up, go to the shop, do my hours, come home and handle whatever needs doing around the house.\n\nAnd I’m still playing ball on weekends, too. Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that, you know? So to me it seems like people are maybe making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess what I see in the mirror feels pretty much like me. I don’t look at it and think, “Oh, that’s a problem,” you know?\n\nI know people are worried, and I’m not saying they’re making it up, but it feels like they’re making more of it than it is. I still get myself to the garage, do my work, come home and take care of whatever needs doing, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like, if I can still do all that, it’s hard for me to see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight affects how you see your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t really think about it like that. When I look at myself, I just see me, you know? I know everyone’s worried and saying stuff, but I keep thinking if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to keep doing my normal things.\n\nLike, I still show up at the shop, get through the day, come home and deal with whatever needs doing around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. Actually, last weekend the guys were joking about how I still run around like I’m younger than I am, so… I don’t know. It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. People keep saying I’m too low or that they’re worried, and I hear them, I do.\n\nBut then I’m like, I still get up and go to work at the shop, I still do what needs doing around the house. I was fixing this one guy’s truck the other day—thing was a mess, honestly—and I got through that fine. And I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to believe it’s as bad as people are making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you appear thinner to yourself than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t really think I look thinner than I am. I mean, people keep saying they’re worried, and I hear it, but I also feel like they might be making too much out of it.\n\nLike, I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. If I was really that bad off, I feel like I wouldn’t be doing all that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the reason people are worried about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, probably that’s part of it. They see the weight or how I look and they get nervous, I guess. But I still feel like they’re making it bigger than it is.\n\nI’m still at the shop every day, you know, getting under cars, dealing with customers, all that. And at home there’s always something that needs fixing or doing, so it’s not like I’m just sitting around unable to function. Plus I still play ball on weekends—last time we were out there one of the guys was complaining more than I was, and he’s younger than me.\n\nSo… yeah, I know they’re worried, but I guess I don’t really see it as being that serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you imagine it does?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. Like, I look in the mirror and I just see… me, I guess. I don’t know if that matches some picture in my head or not.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m working on a car and someone says, “Does that sound normal?” and I’m thinking, normal compared to what, you know? Depends on the engine, depends what it was doing before. So with my body, I don’t know… I don’t walk around with some exact image I’m comparing it to. People say things, but I don’t always see what they’re seeing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I don’t really know if it’s accurate, honestly. I mean, accurate compared to what, you know? I look at myself and it just seems normal to me because it’s what I see every day.

It’s like at the shop—if a car’s been making the same little noise for months, the owner stops noticing it, but then someone else gets in and says, “What’s that sound?” So maybe other people see something I’m not picking up on. I don’t know.

I wouldn’t say I’m trying to see myself wrong or anything. I just… I guess I’m not sure.

At the shop we run late a lot anyway—someone always drops off a car right before closing, you know how that goes—and then at home it’s busy too, so I just… I feel better sticking to my own rules with food. It feels more controlled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a normal or large amount of food would make you feel out of control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Like if I sit down and eat a full plate, especially a big meal, I start feeling like I’ve let things get away from me. So most days I keep it pretty basic—coffee in the morning, then maybe later a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around.\n\nThe shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, so it’s easier to just stick to my own simple rules. I know where I’m at that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit what you eat more about feeling in control or about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably both, but if I’m being honest, the weight thing is what kicks it off. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, sometimes I can see why somebody might say I’m lean, but “too thin” sounds like… I don’t know, like I’d be falling apart or something.\n\nPeople are worried, I get that. But I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff there, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like last weekend we had a pickup game and I was moving fine, so in my head it’s hard not to think people are making a bigger deal out of it than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I mean… healthy, I guess I’d say yes, or at least not as unhealthy as everyone keeps acting. I know people are worried, and I’m not trying to be a jerk about it, but I still get up, go to the shop, do my hours, come home and handle whatever needs doing around the house.\n\nAnd I’m still playing ball on weekends, too. Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that, you know? So to me it seems like people are maybe making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess what I see in the mirror feels pretty much like me. I don’t look at it and think, “Oh, that’s a problem,” you know?\n\nI know people are worried, and I’m not saying they’re making it up, but it feels like they’re making more of it than it is. I still get myself to the garage, do my work, come home and take care of whatever needs doing, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like, if I can still do all that, it’s hard for me to see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight affects how you see your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t really think about it like that. When I look at myself, I just see me, you know? I know everyone’s worried and saying stuff, but I keep thinking if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to keep doing my normal things.\n\nLike, I still show up at the shop, get through the day, come home and deal with whatever needs doing around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. Actually, last weekend the guys were joking about how I still run around like I’m younger than I am, so… I don’t know. It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. People keep saying I’m too low or that they’re worried, and I hear them, I do.\n\nBut then I’m like, I still get up and go to work at the shop, I still do what needs doing around the house. I was fixing this one guy’s truck the other day—thing was a mess, honestly—and I got through that fine. And I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to believe it’s as bad as people are making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you appear thinner to yourself than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t really think I look thinner than I am. I mean, people keep saying they’re worried, and I hear it, but I also feel like they might be making too much out of it.\n\nLike, I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. If I was really that bad off, I feel like I wouldn’t be doing all that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the reason people are worried about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, probably that’s part of it. They see the weight or how I look and they get nervous, I guess. But I still feel like they’re making it bigger than it is.\n\nI’m still at the shop every day, you know, getting under cars, dealing with customers, all that. And at home there’s always something that needs fixing or doing, so it’s not like I’m just sitting around unable to function. Plus I still play ball on weekends—last time we were out there one of the guys was complaining more than I was, and he’s younger than me.\n\nSo… yeah, I know they’re worried, but I guess I don’t really see it as being that serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you imagine it does?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. Like, I look in the mirror and I just see… me, I guess. I don’t know if that matches some picture in my head or not.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m working on a car and someone says, “Does that sound normal?” and I’m thinking, normal compared to what, you know? Depends on the engine, depends what it was doing before. So with my body, I don’t know… I don’t walk around with some exact image I’m comparing it to. People say things, but I don’t always see what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if it’s accurate, honestly. I mean, accurate compared to what, you know? I look at myself and it just seems normal to me because it’s what I see every day.\n\nIt’s like at the shop—if a car’s been making the same little noise for months, the owner stops noticing it, but then someone else gets in and says, “What’s that sound?” So maybe other people see something I’m not picking up on. I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m trying to see myself wrong or anything. I just… I guess I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Maybe. I mean… I get that people are looking at me and thinking I’m too thin or whatever. But I don’t really see it the same way they do.

I still get myself to the garage, do my work, come home and take care of whatever needs doing, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like, if I can still do all that, it’s hard for me to see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight affects how you see your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t really think about it like that. When I look at myself, I just see me, you know? I know everyone’s worried and saying stuff, but I keep thinking if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to keep doing my normal things.\n\nLike, I still show up at the shop, get through the day, come home and deal with whatever needs doing around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. Actually, last weekend the guys were joking about how I still run around like I’m younger than I am, so… I don’t know. It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. Probably not exactly. I mean, people seem to see me and go right to being worried, and I’m looking at myself thinking, I’m still doing what I always do.

It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. People keep saying I’m too low or that they’re worried, and I hear them, I do.\n\nBut then I’m like, I still get up and go to work at the shop, I still do what needs doing around the house. I was fixing this one guy’s truck the other day—thing was a mess, honestly—and I got through that fine. And I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to believe it’s as bad as people are making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you appear thinner to yourself than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t really think I look thinner than I am. I mean, people keep saying they’re worried, and I hear it, but I also feel like they might be making too much out of it.\n\nLike, I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. If I was really that bad off, I feel like I wouldn’t be doing all that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the reason people are worried about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, probably that’s part of it. Like, I look in the mirror and I just see… me, I guess. I don’t know if that matches some picture in my head or not.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m working on a car and someone says, “Does that sound normal?” and I’m thinking, normal compared to what, you know? Depends on the engine, depends what it was doing before. So with my body, I don’t know… I don’t walk around with some exact image I’m comparing it to. People say things, but I don’t always see what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if it’s accurate, honestly. I mean, accurate compared to what, you know? I look at myself and it just seems normal to me because it’s what I see every day.\n\nIt’s like at the shop—if a car’s been making the same little noise for months, the owner stops noticing it, but then someone else gets in and says, “What’s that sound?” So maybe other people see something I’m not picking up on. I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m trying to see myself wrong or anything. I just… I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be underestimating how thin you look to others?\n\nPatient: Maybe. Yeah… I think I would. Even just a few pounds, I’d probably notice it before anybody else did. Like if the scale bumps up one pound, or my jeans feel tighter getting dressed in the morning, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”

And I know that might sound dramatic, but in my line of work, you’re squeezing into weird spots, getting up and down off the floor, moving around cars all day. If I feel heavier, I start thinking I’m gonna be slower, or not move as well. Same with playing ball—up and down the court, you can feel everything.

So yeah, I guess I’d look different to myself pretty quick. Maybe not to anyone else, but to me I’d notice.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, uh, honestly my work physical is what kind of got me sent here. The doctor there said my weight’s too low. I don’t really feel bad about it though—I mean, I feel lighter on my feet when I play basketball, and at the garage I’m not feeling as weighed down moving around cars and equipment and all that. So… I guess they were concerned, but I’m not sure it’s as big a deal as they made it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… “afraid” sounds kind of strong, but yeah, I don’t like it. If I get on the scale and it’s up even a pound, or my jeans feel tighter, I start thinking I’m getting soft. Like I’m gonna be slower getting around at work, crawling around cars, lifting stuff… and on the basketball court too. I know that probably sounds a little much, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you restrict what or how much you eat because of your concerns about weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Most days I just keep it simple, like coffee in the morning, and then maybe later I’ll have a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around. At the shop we run late a lot anyway—someone always drops off a car right before closing, you know how that goes—and then at home it’s busy too, so I just… I feel better sticking to my own rules with food. It feels more controlled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a normal or large amount of food would make you feel out of control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. Like if I sit down and eat a full plate, especially a big meal, I start feeling like I’ve let things get away from me. So most days I keep it pretty basic—coffee in the morning, then maybe later a small sandwich or half of whatever’s around.\n\nThe shop runs late a lot, and then the house is busy when I get home, so it’s easier to just stick to my own simple rules. I know where I’m at that way.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit what you eat more about feeling in control or about not wanting to gain weight?\n\nPatient: It’s… probably both, but if I’m being honest, the weight thing is what kicks it off. Like if the scale bumps up even a pound, or my jeans feel a little tighter, my brain goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.” Then I start thinking I’m gonna be slower moving around cars at work, crawling under stuff, getting up and down, or even running up and down the court when I play basketball.\n\nSo yeah, the control part is there, but it’s mostly me not wanting to gain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is lower than what other people think it is?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess other people think it’s lower than I do. The work physical is actually what got me sent here—the doctor there said my weight was too low. But to me, I don’t really feel “too low,” you know? I feel lighter on my feet, like when I’m playing basketball, and at the garage I don’t feel as weighed down crawling around cars and stuff.\n\nI mean, some of those trucks, you’re squeezing into weird spots all day, and if I feel lighter it just seems like I move better. So… yeah, maybe other people see it different than I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, sometimes I can see why somebody might say I’m lean, but “too thin” sounds like… I don’t know, like I’d be falling apart or something.\n\nPeople are worried, I get that. But I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff there, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like last weekend we had a pickup game and I was moving fine, so in my head it’s hard not to think people are making a bigger deal out of it than it is.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is healthy for someone of your age and height?\n\nPatient: I mean… healthy, I guess I’d say yes, or at least not as unhealthy as everyone keeps acting. I know people are worried, and I’m not trying to be a jerk about it, but I still get up, go to the shop, do my hours, come home and handle whatever needs doing around the house.\n\nAnd I’m still playing ball on weekends, too. Like, if I was really in bad shape, I feel like I wouldn’t be able to do all that, you know? So to me it seems like people are maybe making too much of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess what I see in the mirror feels pretty much like me. I don’t look at it and think, “Oh, that’s a problem,” you know?\n\nI know people are worried, and I’m not saying they’re making it up, but it feels like they’re making more of it than it is. I still get myself to the garage, do my work, come home and take care of whatever needs doing, and I’m still playing ball on weekends. Like, if I can still do all that, it’s hard for me to see it the way they do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight affects how you see your body shape?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but I don’t really think about it like that. When I look at myself, I just see me, you know? I know everyone’s worried and saying stuff, but I keep thinking if it was really that bad, I wouldn’t be able to keep doing my normal things.\n\nLike, I still show up at the shop, get through the day, come home and deal with whatever needs doing around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. Actually, last weekend the guys were joking about how I still run around like I’m younger than I am, so… I don’t know. It just makes it hard for me to see it as this big problem the way other people do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's a difference between how you see your body and how it actually is?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… I guess there could be a difference, but I don’t really see it that way. People keep saying I’m too low or that they’re worried, and I hear them, I do.\n\nBut then I’m like, I still get up and go to work at the shop, I still do what needs doing around the house. I was fixing this one guy’s truck the other day—thing was a mess, honestly—and I got through that fine. And I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to believe it’s as bad as people are making it sound.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you appear thinner to yourself than you actually are?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t really think I look thinner than I am. I mean, people keep saying they’re worried, and I hear it, but I also feel like they might be making too much out of it.\n\nLike, I still get up and go to the shop, do my work, come home and handle stuff around the house. And I still play ball on weekends. If I was really that bad off, I feel like I wouldn’t be doing all that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the reason people are worried about you?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, probably that’s part of it. They see the weight or how I look and they get nervous, I guess. But I still feel like they’re making it bigger than it is.\n\nI’m still at the shop every day, you know, getting under cars, dealing with customers, all that. And at home there’s always something that needs fixing or doing, so it’s not like I’m just sitting around unable to function. Plus I still play ball on weekends—last time we were out there one of the guys was complaining more than I was, and he’s younger than me.\n\nSo… yeah, I know they’re worried, but I guess I don’t really see it as being that serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you imagine it does?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. Like, I look in the mirror and I just see… me, I guess. I don’t know if that matches some picture in my head or not.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m working on a car and someone says, “Does that sound normal?” and I’m thinking, normal compared to what, you know? Depends on the engine, depends what it was doing before. So with my body, I don’t know… I don’t walk around with some exact image I’m comparing it to. People say things, but I don’t always see what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if it’s accurate, honestly. I mean, accurate compared to what, you know? I look at myself and it just seems normal to me because it’s what I see every day.\n\nIt’s like at the shop—if a car’s been making the same little noise for months, the owner stops noticing it, but then someone else gets in and says, “What’s that sound?” So maybe other people see something I’m not picking up on. I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m trying to see myself wrong or anything. I just… I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be underestimating how thin you look to others?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I mean… I get that people are looking at me and thinking I’m too thin or whatever. But I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nTo me, I’m still getting up and going to work every day, crawling around cars, lifting stuff, dealing with whatever comes into the shop. And then I get home and there’s always something—yard, sink, garage door, you know how it is. Plus I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to think it’s as bad as they’re making it sound.\n\nSo maybe I’m underestimating it, I don’t know. But it feels like they’re worrying more than they need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the same way to others as you do to yourself?\n\nPatient: Probably not exactly. I mean, people seem to see me and go right to being worried, and I’m looking at myself thinking, I’m still doing what I always do.\n\nLike, I’m at work every day, and that’s not exactly sitting behind a desk. I’m under cars, moving tires around, dealing with stuck bolts—half the time those things are worse than they should be because somebody rounded them off before they got to us. And then I go home and there’s stuff there too, things that need fixing, whatever. I still play ball on weekends when I can.\n\nSo yeah, maybe they see me different than I see me. But I guess I kind of feel like they’re making more out of it than it is, because if it was really that bad, I don’t know… I feel like I wouldn’t be keeping up like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look different to yourself if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Even just a few pounds, I’d probably notice it before anybody else did. Like if the scale bumps up one pound, or my jeans feel tighter getting dressed in the morning, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd I know that might sound dramatic, but in my line of work, you’re squeezing into weird spots, getting up and down off the floor, moving around cars all day. If I feel heavier, I start thinking I’m gonna be slower, or not move as well. Same with playing ball—up and down the court, you can feel everything.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’d look different to myself pretty quick. Maybe not to anyone else, but to me I’d notice.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… pretty much every day, I guess. Not like I’m sitting there all day staring at a scale, but it comes up.

Like if I step on it and it’s even up a pound, I notice. Or if I’m putting on jeans and they feel a little tighter, then my head starts going, you know, “Great, I’m getting soft.” And then I’m thinking about work, because I’m crawling around cars and reaching into stupid tight spots all day, and if I feel heavier I start thinking I’m gonna be slower or not move right.

Same with playing ball. You’re going up and down the court and you feel every little bit. So yeah, I think about it more than I probably should.

Like I’m gonna be slower getting around at work, crawling around cars, lifting stuff… and on the basketball court too. It feels more controlled that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like eating a normal or large amount of food would make you feel out of control?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, kind of. And at home there’s always something that needs fixing or doing, so it’s not like I’m just sitting around unable to function. Plus I still play ball on weekends—last time we were out there one of the guys was complaining more than I was, and he’s younger than me.\n\nSo… yeah, I know they’re worried, but I guess I don’t really see it as being that serious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body looks the way you imagine it does?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. Like, I look in the mirror and I just see… me, I guess. I don’t know if that matches some picture in my head or not.\n\nIt’s kind of like when I’m working on a car and someone says, “Does that sound normal?” and I’m thinking, normal compared to what, you know? Depends on the engine, depends what it was doing before. So with my body, I don’t know… I don’t walk around with some exact image I’m comparing it to. People say things, but I don’t always see what they’re seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your body image is accurate?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know if it’s accurate, honestly. I mean, accurate compared to what, you know? I look at myself and it just seems normal to me because it’s what I see every day.\n\nIt’s like at the shop—if a car’s been making the same little noise for months, the owner stops noticing it, but then someone else gets in and says, “What’s that sound?” So maybe other people see something I’m not picking up on. I don’t know.\n\nI wouldn’t say I’m trying to see myself wrong or anything. I just… I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you might be underestimating how thin you look to others?\n\nPatient: Maybe. I mean… I get that people are looking at me and thinking I’m too thin or whatever. But I don’t really see it the same way they do.\n\nTo me, I’m still getting up and going to work every day, crawling around cars, lifting stuff, dealing with whatever comes into the shop. And then I get home and there’s always something—yard, sink, garage door, you know how it is. Plus I still play ball on weekends, so it’s hard for me to think it’s as bad as they’re making it sound.\n\nSo maybe I’m underestimating it, I don’t know. But it feels like they’re worrying more than they need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the same way to others as you do to yourself?\n\nPatient: Probably not exactly. I mean, people seem to see me and go right to being worried, and I’m looking at myself thinking, I’m still doing what I always do.\n\nLike, I’m at work every day, and that’s not exactly sitting behind a desk. I’m under cars, moving tires around, dealing with stuck bolts—half the time those things are worse than they should be because somebody rounded them off before they got to us. And then I go home and there’s stuff there too, things that need fixing, whatever. I still play ball on weekends when I can.\n\nSo yeah, maybe they see me different than I see me. But I guess I kind of feel like they’re making more out of it than it is, because if it was really that bad, I don’t know… I feel like I wouldn’t be keeping up like I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would look different to yourself if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. Even just a few pounds, I’d probably notice it before anybody else did. Like if the scale bumps up one pound, or my jeans feel tighter getting dressed in the morning, my head goes right to, “Great, I’m getting soft.”\n\nAnd I know that might sound dramatic, but in my line of work, you’re squeezing into weird spots, getting up and down off the floor, moving around cars all day. If I feel heavier, I start thinking I’m gonna be slower, or not move as well. Same with playing ball—up and down the court, you can feel everything.\n\nSo yeah, I guess I’d look different to myself pretty quick. Maybe not to anyone else, but to me I’d notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… pretty much every day, I guess. Not like I’m sitting there all day staring at a scale, but it comes up.\n\nLike if I step on it and it’s even up a pound, I notice. Or if I’m putting on jeans and they feel a little tighter, then my head starts going, you know, “Great, I’m getting soft.” And then I’m thinking about work, because I’m crawling around cars and reaching into stupid tight spots all day, and if I feel heavier I start thinking I’m gonna be slower or not move right.\n\nSame with playing ball. You’re going up and down the court and you feel every little bit. So yeah, I think about it more than I probably should."
  }
